Wales lost 1-0 to Denmark in their last home Nations League game – they have never lost consecutive home games in the competition, while they last did so in competitive games in defeats to Croatia (1-2) and Serbia (0-3) in March and September 2013 respectively.

Portugal could still finish second should they lose at the Cardiff City Stadium on Tuesday and Denmark beat Norway, but the Selecao will be confident of getting the job done.

They have won 12 of their last 16 competitive away matches (D2 L2), and have lost just three of their last 26 on the road in competitive fixtures (W17 D6).

"It's another very tough test against a very good side but we want to show that we are more than capable of competing with top-quality nations," Wales defender Ben Davies said.

"A couple of games on another day could have gone our way and the next step for us is turning those good performances into results."

While Wales are consigned to a bottom-place finish in the group, they could still avoid relegation from the top tier if they win their relegation play-off in March.

Banijay Unveils First Financials Since All3Media Deal & Says Synergies “Expected To Ramp Up” Next Year

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Banijay has unveiled its first set of results since closing the All3Media deal and said synergies are “expected to ramp up” next year.

The French mega-group posted overall revenue of €4.3B ($4.7B) for the first nine months of 2026, a rapid rise of 33.9% if you take the All3Media and betting firm Tipico acquisitions into account. On a pro-forma basis, the revenue increase was 6.6%. Adjusted EBITDA was up 37.3% to €820M and 3% on a pro-forma basis.

In the Banijay Entertainment content division, production revenue was down 3% pro-forma to €1.6B, while distribution revenue fell around 5%. Overall revenue in the division was up slightly, helped along by the sharply-growing live experiences biz. Profit was up 3.3% pro-forma to €350M. Related Stories News 'The Walking Dead' Star Lennie James Leading ITV Thriller 'Nightswimming' From Harry & Jack Williams News Banijay Rights Names CFO As John Wagener Exits After 20 Years

Banijay said the production and distribution figures “continued to reflect the anticipated seasonality of deliveries and a tough comparison basis in Q2 and Q3 2025, which benefited from major deliveries” like Apple TV’s The Buccaneers and Netflix’s House of Guinness. Watch on Deadline

All3Media, meanwhile, delivered shows in the past nine months like Sky and HBO Max’s War and Prime Video’s revamped Grand Tour.

“This more balanced delivery profile demonstrated one of the benefits of the combination of Banijay Entertainment and All3Media, effective since 9 July 2026, creating a larger and more diversified global entertainment platform,” Banijay said.

The merger completed several weeks ago and integration has begun at pace, with layoffs mainly impacting the sales teams. Banijay today said synergies from both the All3Media and Tipico deals are “expected to ramp up in 2027.” The integration plan is expected to complete early next year, we understand.

“Our latest figures provide the first financial indication of the newly combined Banijay Entertainment – a group that, despite challenging market conditions, continues to demonstrate strength, stability, and a solid standing for the future,” said Banijay CEO Marco Bassetti. “The merger immediately bolstered this business, providing greater creative scale, strengthened English-language output and expanded digital and distribution capabilities. With financial discipline and a robust strategy for the road ahead, we remain positively focused on growth in our core activities and across our burgeoning diversification pillars – live, digital and sport.”

Kin in the Game: How the Rise of Family Office Investors Is Reshaping Media, Entertainment and Sports

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The dawn of Skydance — the film and TV leviathan that came forth from the biggest media acquisition in 25 years — was enabled by the most influential trend in private investment in a decade: the rise of the family office investor.

“Family office” is cuddly financial jargon for a specific form of holding company that ultrarich people and their relatives rely on to manage their wealth. The proliferation of near billionaires — and billionaires proper — in the U.S. since the stock market boom began in the 1980s has led to the creation of thousands of well-endowed family offices. “The size of this is extraordinary,” says Ian D’Souza, clinical professor of finance at the NYU Stern School of Business. “A lot of people don’t realize just how big they are as an investor class in a global context and their different risk-return drivers.” Related Stories 'Nobody Wants This' Renewed for Season 4 at Netflix ‘Russell Brand: Open Secret’: Netflix Doc Reveals His ‘Jokes’ About Rape, Making a Girlfriend ‘Brush Her Teeth Until They Bled’ and More

Family offices are gaining a level of clout that is having a profound impact on the ownership of media and entertainment assets. Many operate with a low profile — they don’t have the same financial disclosure requirements as other companies and investment firms. Among the most powerful entities flexing financial muscle is Lawrence Investments, the office that handles the prodigious fortune amassed by 82-year-old Larry Ellison, co-founder of software giant Oracle. Ellison is the father of Skydance chairman and CEO David Ellison, the mastermind of Skydance’s quest to bolt together studio and network assets.

The younger Ellison’s one-two punch began last year with his $8 billion acquisition of Paramount and CBS. And then he went in dogged pursuit of Warner Bros., HBO, CNN, Discovery and numerous other channels. It all culminated in the closing of the $111 billion Warner Bros. Discovery transaction on Oct. 6. David Ellison couldn’t have pulled off those head-spinning deals without the full faith and credit backing of the enormous Ellison family wealth held through Lawrence Investments, which has an estimated $160 billion in assets under management. But Ellison’s empire-building boom has been financed with debt.

The enlarged Skydance is carrying $80 billion in red ink, and if Skydance doesn’t make its aggressive debt and cost-reduction targets over the next three years, Larry Ellison will personally be on the hook for as much as $40 billion in debt payments to lenders. That’s how far the software mogul, who recently became a major shareholder in the U.S. spinoff of TikTok, went to help finance his son’s Hollywood ambition.

As demonstrated by the Ellison family’s big swing, the influence of family office owners and investors will be markedly different from that of traditional private equity investors, experts say. Among the key differences:

According to data analysis from global investor research firm Altss, the number of family offices has grown at a rate of nearly 5% a year, culminating in more than 8,000 firms managing as much as $5 trillion or more in assets. The top 50 firms have an estimated $2.4 trillion in assets under management; the top 10 alone account for $1.5 trillion.

Generally speaking, it takes about $250 million in seed money to launch a large single-investor family office. Others are formed with $50 million to $100 million, and sometimes a handful of those firms will join together in a multi-family office. One of the biggest of those is Iconiq Capital, which handles some personal investing for Mark Zuckerberg, Sheryl Sandberg, Jack Dorsey and Linkedin co-founder Reid Hoffman, among others.

The reigning company among family offices remains Walton Enterprises, the $225 billion colossus controlled by the heirs of Walmart founder Sam Walton. Elon Musk, the entrepreneur behind Tesla and SpaceX, funnels some of his personal investments through his Excession family office, valued at $200 billion. Bill Gates’ Cascade Investment is another heavyweight at $170 billion. Amazon pioneer Jeff Bezos holds his roughly 9% stake in the e-commerce giant (which encompasses Amazon MGM Studios and Prime Video) through his Bezos Expeditions office, with an estimated $107 billion in assets under management.

“The ability to create wealth over a short period of time right now is incredible, and because of that, all of a sudden you’ve got someone like Elon buying Twitter and turning it into what he wanted it to be,” says Greg Suess, co-founder and head of Activist Artists Management’s family office practice. “It was unfathomable that you would see an individual be able to buy a company of that size on his own. But that is the way it is now. A lot of these family offices are of the size where they can take swings on major entertainment assets. That has shocked the industry.”

At the same time, fortunes the size of Ellison’s, Musk’s and the Walton clan’s are still outliers — the 1% of the 1%. The more pervasive impact is coming from the many other family offices steered by less than astronomically wealthy people who are drawn to the soft power of media. Think Steve Sarowitz, the payroll-software billionaire whose Illinois-based 4S Bay Partners family office teamed with Justin Baldoni to launch “It Ends With Us” production company Wayfarer Studios.

David Steward II has also inherited significant wealth from his father, World Wide Technology co-founder David Steward. The younger Steward in recent years has invested in comic-book imprints and the Lion Forge Entertainment animation studio with the goal of bringing more diverse stories to life as family animation films. The Steward family has also worked with NASCAR and Richard Petty Motorsports to sponsor the groundbreaking driver Bubba Wallace, and it was an owner of the family’s hometown NHL team, the St. Louis Blues, from 2012 until selling the stake in 2024.

“Our mission is very much around what we call diverse stories authentically told. It’s about displaying and telling stories from varied backgrounds, whether it be racial, socioeconomic or gender,” the younger Steward says. “We’re a lot more patient with outcomes because we have a higher vision. We’re not only investing to get a return; we’re investing for change. We’re investing in people at the end of the day. So it’s a different kind of philosophy as we look at those things.

“There are three pillars to any family office that I’ve ever been around,” says Edward Hamati, a wealth management pro who is chief investment officer for the Steward Trust and the Steward Family Office. “Trust, treasury and banking — that’s one bucket. Building the corpus, that’s another bucket. Anyone can give you 50 ways that you do that. And then there’s the third bucket, which is, What is this family about and what do we have to care about because of the family? What are they motivated by? What do the kids want? Is there a lot of family politics? You have to think through all that around any family you work with.”

Joe Becher, CAA’s global managing partner and founder of its executive search practice, has felt the same demand that spurred Suess and partners to form Activist. Becher expanded CAA into family office specialty services as he found himself conducting C-suite searches for firms that had been bought out by family offices.

“Probably two to three years ago we started seeing this trend of family offices popping up everywhere, whether it be in media companies or music-catalog businesses or sports franchises,” Becher says. “Very often, instead of us dealing with an owner in isolation or a private equity firm with institutional capital, we were dealing with family offices. They needed our help to understand the assets that they’ve invested in. They needed our help to structure them the right way and build the right operating teams.”

Traditionally, the family office investment model was associated with vintage American family fortunes such as the oil money of the Rockefellers or the publishing and media empires of William Randolph Hearst and E.W. Scripps. Hearst and Scripps still operate as well-established media companies. But their governance structure makes them essentially family offices that are run by trusted staffers who ensure that the generational wealth keeps flowing to heirs for decades to come.

But nothing has animated the family office sector lately like the availability of sports teams and music catalogs. The latter is seen as a form of timeless physical media that will retain value no matter how much music consumption changes in the streaming era. On the other hand, sports franchise deals are usually driven by raw passion and bragging rights.

“There’s only so many of them, and so that sense of paying for scarcity and legacy impact are important drivers for family offices that don’t really exist for other types of pooled capital because they have a time-duration problem,” D’Souza says. Some first-generation wealth generators thrive on the challenge of turning around properties like sports teams or rehabilitating beloved fashion or retail brands. “They feel like, ‘I made it once; I can do it again,’” says D’Souza, who has served as a board member for numerous family offices over the years.

At best, a family office can bring to the table resources and in some cases synergies with other holdings for an acquisition. Steward II notes that the company’s work with NASCAR and driver Wallace led to World Wide Technology upgrading the racing circuit’s digital infrastructure to help it make the most of AI and other cutting-edge tech.

Steward II emphasizes that the company can’t completely ignore financial results. But it has the luxury of options. “We do have the investments that are a little bit colder and distant and more financially oriented. And then we have this entrepreneurial pile, and in that pile comes [ideas] by way of passion and by way of utilizing the relationships that we have,” Steward says. “Everything we’ve done in sports and entertainment has been led by those two things. We want to see change in the industry as well. So how can we best not only put our creative foot forward, but how can we leverage our relationships for change as well?”

The flood of investment and financing dollars flowing into a broad range of industry deals in recent years has turned the private investment vehicles of the ultra-high-net-worth crowd into the most courted players in global investing. “It has become recognized that families are more viable owners of various assets than they were considered to be in the past,” Activist’s Suess says. “The wealth has grown so much. They’ve become business rock stars.”

(Pictured top: Family office investors Gigi Pritzker, Steve Sarowitz, David Steward II and François-Henri Pinault)

Fabregas la bellezza oltre la vittoria: meritava la panchina d'oro

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

“ Chivu al primo anno all’Inter è stato bravissimo soprattutto dopo il 5-0 con PSG, perché ha ricostruito il gruppo. In campionato poi aveva la squadra e ha vinto con merito, ma il lavoro di Fabregas l’anno scorso è stato straordinario. Aver portato il Como in Champions giocando in quella maniera, meritava un riconoscimento. La panchina d'oro andava a lui.” A Coverciano è stata assegnata la panchina d’oro a Chivu per la stagione 25-26, ma Massimo Orlando a Maracanà è stato di tutt’altro opinione.

Un riconoscimento che ha diviso opinionisti e ascoltatori del resto stiamo parlando di due tecnici che hanno fatto un gran lavoro, certificato anche dai voti. Le 4 preferenze in più per Chivu nella votazione finale fa capire quanto il confronto sia stato equilibrato. Chivu ha guidato la squadre più forte che qualcuno ha paragonato ad una macchina di formula uno, ma attenzione per guidare un vettura del genere devi essere molto bravo e il tecnico dell’Inter lo ha dimostrato. Prima ha rigenerato una squadra reduce da una stagione con zero titoli e poi ha rimesso in corsa l’Inter centrando campionato e coppa Italia. Direi non male per un tecnico con 13 panchine in Serie A prima di approdare all’Inter.

Un grande lavoro ha fatto anche Fabregas con il Como. Valorizzazione della rosa, calcio completo capace attaccare senza dimenticare la fase offensiva e poi ciliegina sulla torta raggiungimento della zona Champions. Un obiettivo che può essere paragonato per il Como ad uno scudetto. Un’impresa storica difficile da pronosticare ad inizio stagione. Avere dei dubbi sull’assegnazione della panchina d’oro della scorsa stagione, visto i risultati di entrambi i tecnici è più che legittimo.

Ma fare 0-0 non è mia abitudine e allora questa volta dico di essere d’accordo con il pensiero iniziale di Massimo Orlando. Il Como l’anno scorso ha compiuto qualcosa di unico, la squadra lariana e stata la grande bellezza del calcio. Vedere questa squadra arrivare in zona in Champions praticando un gioco meraviglioso meritava un riconoscimento per Fabregas. Si può lasciare il segno anche senza vincere un trofeo, come fece l’Olanda degli anni 70. Vincere non è l’unica cosa che conta.

Casey Hudson still finds it hard to comprehend

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Personally, I still haven't wrapped my head around BioWare's closure. The legendary home of Mass Effect, Star Wars: Knights of the Old Republic, and Dragon Age was a standard-bearer in modern RPG design for so long; it seemed like it could weather any storm. Alas, the industry right now is difficult, but even former leadership is struggling to comprehend this turn of events.

On The Critical Path podcast, Casey Hudson, former BioWare general manager and creator of the original Mass Effect, explained where his head's at as the company closes. "Part of the reason I came back and wanted to be GM of the studio, and was interested in that, was because I've just got so many friends there and I love the studio," he says.

And I wanted to do whatever I could in the years I was there to try and set it up for some kind of longevity," he explains. Hudson left BioWare twice: first exiting in 2014 and winding up at Microsoft for a spell, then returning to upper management in 2017 before leaving again in 2020.

"There are things that you think are just going to last forever," he adds. "But I think that even though we all worried that the day would come when it would get shut down, it's still a different feeling to realize that it is actually closed."

I suspect the notion of an unceremonious end lingered in the air quite a few times, especially over the last ten years or so. You had the rocky reception to Mass Effect: Andromeda, and the years it took to make Dragon Age: The Veilguard, only for the game to get a muted response. These are the kinds of things that can make any studio unsustainable.

But it always seemed like BioWare had another banger in it, and what we've heard about Mass Effect 5 indicated the team had the right ideas. Hudson mentions that he's among the chorus wondering if we'll ever get some version of that game. EA could sell the franchise to someone else, but it may hoard the dormant IP, too.

"It's very difficult," he comments. "Especially for folks that I know that are on that team. I think everyone's just hoping there's some way forward, both individually for folks in their careers, but also for the great games that BioWare made."

What Do We Do With Leftover Love?

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 16, 2026 | Reviewed by Ekua Hagan

He was everything you had ever wanted. Kind, gentle, thoughtful. Almost too good to be true. You imagined a future together, a home, a family, a lifetime of ordinary mornings. Then, one day, while making eggs for breakfast, you saw the messages. The pan was still warm. The sun was coming through the window. Nothing around you had changed, yet it's all gone. In a matter of seconds, the man you thought you knew disappeared. The future you had imagined vanished with him. But the love you felt still lingers.

You can throw the eggs into the bin, but the feelings cannot disappear as quickly. Love involves several interconnected brain systems. Dopamine-rich reward pathways contribute to desire and anticipation (Fisher et al., 2010); the brain's natural opioid system helps make closeness comforting and social rejection distressing (Hsu et al., 2013; Nummenmaa et al., 2016); and limbic regions, particularly the amygdala, help connect emotional experiences with memory (Cahill et al., 1996). The truth can change what you think in seconds, but it cannot instantly erase what your brain has learned over months or years, so do not let people tell you that you are "co-dependent" or "weak." You have a normal brain that might play tricks on you.

You have leftover love, and that leftover love can be weaponised against you (Lesiak & Gelsthorpe, 2025). He may use the feelings that remain to persuade you to let him stay, asking you to ignore what you have discovered, silence your judgment, and compromise the very values that define who you are.

He has already betrayed you. Now he is asking you to betray yourself for him.

You are the one expected to swallow your anger, silence your judgment, and call it "forgiveness." Look at the bargain. He gets to keep the woman who loves him. And you are expected to give up the woman who knows the truth.

Decades ago, psychiatrist Judith Herman wrote about it in Trauma and Recovery (1992):

His ultimate goal appears to be the creation of a willing victim. Hostages, political prisoners, battered women, and slaves have all remarked upon the captor’s curious psychological dependence upon his victim. George Orwell gives voice to the totalitarian mind in the novel 1984:

“We are not content with negative obedience, nor even with the most abject submission. When finally you surrender to us, it must be of your own free will. We do not destroy the heretic because he resists us; so long as he resists us we never destroy him. We convert him, we capture his inner mind, we reshape him. We burn all evil and all illusion out of him; we bring him over to our side, not in appearance, but genuinely, heart and soul.”

Read those last words again. Heart and soul.

It is not enough that you stay. You must want to stay. It is not enough that you forgive him. You must learn to see his betrayal as something you can live with. You must look at the evidence of your own eyes and persuade yourself that it no longer matters.

And if keeping him means abandoning everything you believe in, what exactly are you trying to save—and what's left—when you do that?

Some wounds are easier to bear because you know you stood by your values while receiving them. In Kraków, officers Jakub Kulik and Artur Morański were seriously wounded confronting a mass shooter. Poland calls them heroes, and people across the country are grateful for their courage and service.

Then there is another kind of wound. One that no surgeon can stitch. Amy Winehouse sings it in "You Know I'm No Good": “I cheated myself, like I knew I would.” By the end of the song, she is no longer simply confessing what she did. She is passing judgment on herself.

Imagine waking up beside a man you no longer respect, living a life you never wanted, having persuaded yourself to accept things you once knew were unacceptable. You kept him, perhaps. But somewhere along the way, you lost the ability to trust your own judgment.

That is the deeper cruelty of weaponised love (Lesiak & Gelsthorpe, 2025). He does not need you to believe he is innocent. He needs you to stop caring that he isn't.

And if manipulation or coercion brought you to that point, the shame belongs to the person who engineered that surrender, not to you.

Name the man, not the fantasy. The betrayal wasn't simply another woman. It was the months of lies that made you believe you were building a future together while he was living another life. He needed you to trust him because, had you known the truth, you might have walked away. So he made sure you didn't.

That is what a programme of deception does. It robs you of the freedom to make informed choices about your own life. When used to dominate and subordinate, deception becomes a tool of coercive control and psychological abuse. Stop reaching for words that make him sound better. A liar is a liar. A traitor is a traitor. A backstabber is a backstabber. Grieve the man he pretended to be, the image he created to win your love and use it to control you. But don't defend the man behind the mask.

The eggs went into the bin that morning. The relationship ended. The feelings are disappearing. You'll be ok! Probably you are already more ok now than when you were cheated on, lied to, and abused.

Herman, J. L. (1992). Trauma and recovery: The aftermath of violence—From domestic abuse to political terror. Basic Books. https://ia803207.us.archive.org/14/items/radfem-books/Trauma%20and%20Recovery_%20The%20Afterm%20-%20Judith%20L.%20Herman.pdf

Lesiak, M., & Gelsthorpe, L. (2025). The invisible abuser: Attachment, victimization, and perpetrator perception in repeat abuse. Violence Against Women. https://doi.org/10.1177/10778012251379423

Best Bitcoin Staking Platforms in 2026: BTC Rewards, Custody and Risks Compared

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Log in to access your notifications and stay updated. If you’re not a member yet, Sign Up to get started!

Sara is steadily working on cryptocurrency evaluations, news, and fluctuations in digital currency prices. She is guest author associated with many cryptocurrencies admin and contributes as an active guide to readers about recent updates on virtual currencies.

Sara is steadily working on cryptocurrency evaluations, news, and fluctuations in digital currency prices. She is guest author associated with many cryptocurrencies admin and contributes as an active guide to readers about recent updates on virtual currencies.

Sara is steadily working on cryptocurrency evaluations, news, and fluctuations in digital currency prices. She is guest author associated with many cryptocurrencies admin and contributes as an active guide to readers about recent updates on virtual currencies.

Bitcoin staking allows holders to earn rewards on their BTC without selling the underlying asset. In 2026, Stacks, Babylon, Core, Starknet and Stacking DAO offer different approaches, with varying rewards, custody models and staking requirements.

Stacks targets approximately 3% annualized rewards in BTC, while Babylon, Core and Starknet distribute rewards in their native tokens. Stacking DAO offers liquid staking with BTC-linked returns.

Choosing a platform requires looking beyond advertised yields to understand token requirements, custody arrangements and withdrawal conditions.

Rates as of November 10, 2026. Stacks quotes a target annual yield, Babylon quotes APR, and other displayed rates may use different calculation conventions.

The main differences concern reward sources, custody arrangements, additional capital requirements and withdrawal flexibility.

Bitcoin staking rewards vary by source, denomination and additional capital requirements.

Stacks is a Bitcoin layer where BTC holders earn BTC.

Stacks miners commit BTC through Proof of Transfer (PoX) to compete for the right to produce Stacks blocks and earn STX rewards. The committed BTC is distributed to staking participants, providing the source of their BTC rewards. PoX has distributed over 4,200 BTC since January 2021.

Direct Bitcoin staking targets approximately 3% annualized yield, with weekly BTC rewards. Since September 2026, three staking bonds have sold out, bringing total BTC staked to 1,740 BTC.

Babylon distributes BABY rewards funded by newly issued tokens through the protocol’s inflation mechanism. Its displayed APR ranges from 0.04% to 0.52%, with the higher rate requiring participants to co-stake BABY alongside BTC.

Returns depend on reward distribution and BABY’s value relative to Bitcoin.

Core distributes CORE rewards to participants who delegate BTC to validators securing its blockchain. Rewards come from newly issued CORE block rewards and transaction fees.

Its displayed top-tier yield is 0.79%, with higher reward tiers requiring participants to stake CORE alongside BTC.

Starknet allows holders of supported tokenized Bitcoin assets to delegate them to validators securing the network.

Participants earn newly minted STRK tokens, with a displayed yield of 2.91%. Actual returns depend on network participation and STRK’s value relative to BTC.

Stacking DAO provides liquid Bitcoin staking through stBTC, a token representing an sBTC-based staking position. BTC rewards generated through Bitcoin staking on Stacks accumulate in the position, increasing the amount of sBTC backing each stBTC over time.

Its displayed yield is 2.31%, with rewards accruing automatically rather than requiring separate claims.

A 4% annual reward paid in a token that loses 50% of its value against BTC would be worth approximately 2% of the original BTC position, assuming other factors remain unchanged.

BTC rewards avoid this specific reward-token conversion risk, although staking still involves other costs and risks.

Stacks, Babylon and Core support native Bitcoin staking on Bitcoin L1, while Starknet and Stacking DAO use Bitcoin-backed assets on other layers.

In direct self-custodial staking, BTC remains timelocked on Bitcoin L1 under the participant’s custody. Institutions can also access this route through qualified custody infrastructure, including BitGo and Anchorage Digital.

BTC is locked on Bitcoin L1 and delegated to finality providers supporting the Babylon ecosystem. Bitcoin remains native, but the staking position is subject to protocol slashing conditions.

BTC holders timelock their Bitcoin on L1 and delegate staking weight to Core validators without transferring BTC to another chain.

Participants stake supported tokenized Bitcoin assets on Starknet, introducing dependencies on the underlying token’s backing and smart contracts.

Participants can deposit sBTC, a Bitcoin-backed asset on Stacks, or BTC, which is converted into sBTC. In return, they receive stBTC, representing their staking position and accruing BTC rewards. Part of Stacks’ Bitcoin staking capacity is allocated to this pooled staking route. Unlike direct staking on Stacks, this route is not self-custodial.

Native Bitcoin custody avoids certain bridge and wrapper dependencies but does not remove staking-specific risks.

Some protocols require additional token commitments, while others use them to increase rewards.

Stacks uses STX as staking capacity. Direct participants commit STX worth approximately 5% of their BTC position, locked for the staking term.

Babylon offers increased rewards through BABY co-staking, while Core uses CORE dual staking to determine reward tiers.

Starknet does not require participants to pair STRK with their BTC for standard delegation. Stacking DAO handles the underlying STX commitment without requiring participants to provide it separately.

These requirements affect capital efficiency because additional tokens introduce further price exposure.

Withdrawal conditions vary across staking models.

Stacks uses approximately six-month bonding periods for direct staking. Participants can exit early and recover BTC, but forfeit remaining rewards, while paired STX stays locked until the original term ends.

Babylon supports unbonding, subject to protocol-defined waiting periods and transaction requirements.

Core allows participants to choose how long to timelock their BTC on Bitcoin L1, with a minimum staking period of 24 hours. Once the term expires, BTC can be redeemed through a Bitcoin transaction.

Starknet allows participants to unstake tokenized BTC, subject to a seven-day withdrawal period during which rewards stop accruing. Liquid staking providers may offer alternative exit routes.

Stacking DAO supports instant stBTC-to-sBTC withdrawals when sufficient liquidity is available, subject to fees. Alternatively, participants can use a standard withdrawal with an approximately four-week cooldown. Converting sBTC into native BTC requires a separate withdrawal process.

Liquid staking provides flexibility, but instant redemption depends on available liquidity and protocol conditions.

Bitcoin staking risks depend on custody, reward assets and protocol conditions.

Token exposure: Stacks requires STX for direct staking, while Babylon and Core offer higher rewards through BABY and CORE commitments. Token price changes affect overall returns.

Slashing: Babylon can penalize staked BTC when delegated finality providers violate specified security conditions. Stacks’ self-custodial path does not apply BTC slashing.

Smart contracts: Starknet and Stacking DAO rely on tokenized Bitcoin infrastructure, introducing smart contract and asset-backing risks.

Liquidity and lockups: Native staking can restrict withdrawals, while liquid staking positions depend on redemption mechanisms and market liquidity.

Quoted yields should therefore be evaluated alongside custody arrangements, token requirements and withdrawal conditions.

Stacks is a strong option for eligible holders prioritizing BTC rewards and native custody. Babylon and Core offer alternative native staking models, while Starknet and Stacking DAO provide tokenized Bitcoin staking options.

Among the quoted figures, Stacks has the highest stated rate at approximately 3% target annual yield, followed by Starknet at 2.91%. Reward denominations and calculation methods differ, so these rates are not directly comparable.

Yes. Stacks distributes BTC rewards through direct Bitcoin staking, while Stacking DAO provides BTC-linked rewards through stBTC. Babylon, Core and Starknet distribute rewards in their respective native tokens.

Yes. Stacks, Babylon and Core support native BTC staking on Bitcoin L1. Their mechanisms differ in token requirements, withdrawal conditions and slashing exposure.

Risks include reward-token volatility, additional capital requirements, slashing, lockups, smart contract vulnerabilities and liquidity constraints. The relevant risks depend on the platform and staking method.

Prediction: Nvidia Stock Will Beat the Market Over the Next 10 Years

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Jensen Huang stepped away from the company. I’m still not selling.

Nvidia (NVDA -3.60%) is arguably navigating one of the most challenging periods in the company's history. The tech leader's co-founder and longtime CEO, Jensen Huang, stepped down from his role after it was revealed that he had been unfaithful to his wife with at least two women, including one who is rumored to have been a Chinese spy.

Huang did a wonderful job of leading Nvidia and making it one of the most successful and powerful companies in the world, given its position in the artificial intelligence (AI) value chain. But the stock has declined by 28% over the past month. It could sink even further. Despite all that, I am not planning to sell my shares. In fact, I still think Nvidia could beat the market over the next decade.

There is a reason that Wall Street loves founder-led businesses. Some of the largest and most successful companies in the world became so due in large part to the leadership and vision of their legendary founders. The list includes Apple (AAPL -0.89%), Amazon (AMZN -3.17%), Microsoft (MSFT -0.30%), and Berkshire Hathaway (BRKA -0.04%) (BRKB +0.01%), among others. We can add Nvidia to this list.

However, notice that some of the corporations above performed well long after their founder CEO left. For instance, Bill Gates stepped down as CEO of Microsoft in 2000. The tech company has crushed broader equities ever since. Similarly, Steve Jobs stepped down as Apple's leader in 2011, and the company has performed extremely well ever since. Of course, that's no guarantee that the same will happen to Nvidia.

But while Huang's leadership was instrumental in getting the company to where it is today, Nvidia's business, as it stands, has certain strengths that will survive the departure of its CEO. Nvidia boasts the leading share in the GPU (graphics processing unit) market, technological expertise, a vast portfolio of patents and copyrights, and the CUDA ecosystem -- perhaps its greatest strength -- that grants it a competitive advantage from switching costs.

None of these will simply evaporate. Further, Nvidia arguably has several other high-level executives who know the business inside and out and can do at least a decent job of leading the company through its next chapter.

Ian Buck, the man widely credited with much of the technical work behind the development of CUDA, is one of them. He has been with the company for more than two decades and is currently the vice president and general manager of hyperscale and high-performance computing.

Colette Kress has stepped up as interim CEO. She was previously the company's CFO and has been with the company for over a decade. Perhaps the board will choose someone from outside the company to replace Huang, although the pool of legitimate candidates is probably very small.

Earnings season provided strong evidence that the AI tailwind is far from over. Some hyperscalers continue to spend fortunes on infrastructure, while demand for compute capacity is soaring, as the financial results of neoclouds such as CoreWeave (CRWV -6.49%) showed.

Nvidia remains in an excellent position to capitalize on this over the long run. True, Huang's ability to identify the next potentially massive opportunity will be sorely missed. Even so, he did a wonderful job of transforming Nvidia into a corporation that provides not just GPUs, but a comprehensive, end-to-end AI infrastructure platform. It's also worth emphasizing that Nvidia's financial success in recent years could help it navigate the next chapter.

The company generated $127 billion in free cash flow over the trailing-12-month period. Nvidia has so much cash that it has significantly increased dividends -- by 2,400% -- and share buybacks this year. Nvidia benefits from significant financial flexibility, an asset that investors shouldn't ignore.

It's for all those reasons (and others) that I am not considering selling my shares. I am bullish on the long-term prospects of AI, and I am also convinced that Nvidia's board will do its best to find an excellent replacement for Huang.

Combined with the company's already excellent position in the industry, the stock could still deliver outstanding returns over the next decade, although there is more uncertainty now than there was a week ago. The good news is that the market has adjusted. Nvidia's shares aren't that far from their 52-week lows, and at current levels, the stock looks attractive to hold through 2036.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 823%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 209% for the S&P 500.

They just revealed what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy right now, available when you join Stock Advisor.

When did Scotland last beat Switzerland? Full head to head record and last five games

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Scotland’s Nations League campaign wraps up on Monday evening with a tough encounter against Switzerland as the side aims to snatch an unlikely promotion playoff spot in Group B1 from Slovenia.

Head coach Sebastien Pocognoli finally got his first home win with a 2-1 win over North Macedonia on Friday, and now his team will look to make it back-to-back wins for the first time under his management while hoping Slovenia slip up in the group’s other game.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

However, should Scotland fail to win in St Gallen, hopes of promotion back to League A will be extinguished. A third-place finish will also have ramifications for the upcoming Euro 2028 qualifiers, which are drawn in Belfast this December.

But when did Scotland last beat Switzerland? What is their head-to-head record, and when did they last meet? Here’s everything you need to know about Scotland’s record against Switzerland.

The teams have faced each other a total of 18 times, and have met twice in major tournament finals, with both encounters taking place in the group stage of the European Championships.

They last faced each other just last month in the Nations League, where Switzerland ran out 3-0 winners after Jack Hendry was sent off early on at Hampden Park. Prior to that game, they drew 1-1 at Euro 2024 in Germany, with Scott McTominay opening the scoring early on for Scotland, before Xherdan Shaqiri equalised shortly afterwards.

From their 18 games played, Scotland have eight wins, giving them a 33.3% win percentage. Switzerland have won a total of six times - a 22.22 per cent win percentage. A further four games have ended in a draw.

Despite having the better head to head record against the Swiss, the Scotland men’s national team have not tasted victory against them for over 30 years. Their most recent win came at Euro 1996 at Villa Park, Birmingham on 18 June, where they earned their first win a 1-0 win in their final group game at the tournament.

The victory came via a fine strike from Ally McCoist in the 36th minute, but was not enough for Scotland to qualify for the knockout stages of the tournament, with the Netherlands finishing above them on goal difference. The Switzerland side that day included future Celtic defender Ramon Vega.

Scotland line up that night: Andy Goram, Tom Boyd, Colin Calderwood, Colin Hendry, Stuart McCall, Gary McAllister, John Collins, Tosh McKinlay (Scott Booth, 60), Craig Burley, Gordon Durie and Ally McCoist (John Spencer, 84).

19 June 2024: Scotland 1-1 Switzerland (Euro 2024)

1 March 2006: Scotland 1-3 Switzerland (Friendly)

18 June 1996: Scotland 1-0 Switzerland (Euro 1996)

8 September 1993: Scotland 1-1 Switzerland (World Cup 1994 Qualifier)

52 Years Later, One Of Sci-Fi's Most Legendary Space Operas Finally Gets Its Streaming Finale

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

One of pop culture's longest-running space operas celebrates the end of an era. The space opera genre is most famously represented by Star Wars, the movie-turned-franchise that overhauled sci-fi cinema with its revolutionary use of VFX and SFX before creating a massive multimedia empire that only continues to grow half a century later. Dune became a phenomenon of its own earlier than Star Wars, though it only met its full potential thirty-seven years after its first movie adaptation and fifty-six years after the first novel's publication. On television, Star Trek pioneered optimistic cosmic exploration and Battlestar Galactica brought survival-driven drama to extraterrestrial fleet warfare.

Tucked beneath those household names, dozens of underrated space operas still await the mainstream recognition they deserve. The Jim Henson Company's Farscape is a unique title in the genre due to its high-quality puppetry, and Babylon 5 popularized serialized five-year television storytelling long before the streaming era. In animation, Titan A.E., Crest of the Stars, and Outlaw Star delivered heartfelt starfaring adventures that often get overshadowed by bigger franchises. There are also countless literary gems like Vernor Vinge’s A Fire Upon the Deep, Lois McMaster Bujold’s Vorkosigan Saga, and Arkady Martine’s A Memory Called Empire.

Right alongside Star Wars and Star Trek, a fan-favorite space opera reaches a new milestone half a century after its first installment.

Fifty-two years after the original 1974 series, one of anime's most influential space operas has delivered its complete streaming finale with all twenty-six episodes of Star Blazers: Space Battleship Yamato 3199. The series' final chapter weaves together the 1980 theatrical feature Be Forever Yamato and the third classic television season, Space Battleship Yamato III, into a cohesive new timeline. Set in the year 2207, Star Blazers: Space Battleship Yamato 3199 follows Susumu Kodai and the scattered veterans of the Yamato as they reunite in secret after a colossal structure called the Grand Reverse breaches Earth's defenses and allows the enigmatic Dezarium army to occupy the capital.

Star Blazers: Space Battleship Yamato 3199 presents a striking blend of classic mechanical designs and mature military drama that respects decades of canon and keeps veteran fans guessing at every turn, plus sweeping orchestral music that makes the space opera feel even more majestic. The seventh and final chapter adds episodes 23 through 26 to the catalog after its October 2026 theatrical debut in Japan. With the entire twenty-six-episode arc all available at once, newcomers and longtime admirers alike can experience the complete saga without interruption after more than fourteen years of anticipation.

Star Blazers: Space Battleship Yamato 3199 brings classic space opera back to its roots with every classic space opera trope that originally inspired modern sci-fi icons like Star Wars and Battlestar Galactica. Throughout its seven chapters, 3199 delivers daring espionage behind enemy lines and thundering fleet battles led by captain Susumu Kodai, tactical ace Yuki Mori, veteran tactician Shiro Sanada, and the rapidly growing Sasha alongside the menacing Dezarium commander Alphon. Standout installments like the tense opening invasion in Chapter 1, the underground resistance intrigue of Chapter 3, and the breathtaking battles in Chapters 6 and 7 showcase breathtaking choreography and Akira Miyagawa's lush orchestral score.

If you love Star Trek, Captain Kirk’s ongoing series needs to be on your radar, especially if you’re a lore fanatic.

Now that all twenty-six episodes of 3199 have wrapped up the series, the future of the Space Battleship Yamato franchise looks remarkably bright as it enters its second half-century. Since launching the modern remake continuity with Yamato 2199 in 2012, studios like Xebec, Satelight, and Studio Mother have reimagined the classic 1970s and early-1980s saga to immense critical and commercial success. Given the warm reception to 3199 and chief director Harutoshi Fukui's clear passion for the material, a new installment could present a modern reimagining of the 1983 theatrical epic Final Yamato and its legendary Planet Aquarius crisis alongside the upcoming movie Cosmoship Yamato √2199.

Charlie Harper's Two And A Half Men Return Made Things So Much Worse

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Leading up to the long-overdue end of Two and a Half Men, there was rampant speculation about whether or not Charlie Harper would return. Midway through the eighth season, Charlie Sheen had been fired from the show for substance abuse issues, and the producers charged on for four more seasons with Ashton Kutcher as their new lead. There were ups and downs in the Kutcher years, but let’s face it: Two and a Half Men died with Charlie, and they should’ve just cut their losses and ended the show after Sheen’s departure.

In its final season, Two and a Half Men finally started to claw its way back to its original premise of dysfunctional co-parenting, as Walden adopted a little boy and he and Alan clashed on how to raise him. But in its final episode, it threw that storyline out the window so that co-creator Chuck Lorre could rehash his public feud with Sheen, four years on.

All throughout the final episode, we get hints that Charlie’s death was faked, and he’s come back for revenge (not on the stalker who trapped him in a well for the past four years, but on the family who loved and supported him and never gave up on him — that makes sense!). In the end, Charlie Harper made an appearance, but Charlie Sheen didn’t. A bowling-shirt-clad body double, shown from behind, walks up to the front door of the beach house, rings the doorbell, and gets flattened by a falling piano.

Lorre then breaks the fourth wall to say, “Winning!,” before a second piano falls on him. It torched the legacy of the show, and the last remaining shred of goodwill in the fan base that stuck around for 12 years, in the name of a petty rivalry that had been put to bed years earlier.

Charlie’s “return” in the Two and a Half Men finale was in extremely poor taste. Lorre reopened his old feud with Sheen without giving Sheen the opportunity to give his side. He spends the entire episode torturing Sheen’s character, then drags him out of the well to crush him with a piano. After the credits, Lorre’s vanity card reveals that Sheen refused to return for the finale because he found that ending for his character to be insulting, which is perfectly understandable.

We all predicted that Two and a Half Men was going to do something spiteful and vindictive with Charlie’s character, but even with that expectation, the finale went over the line. Just the fact that Two and a Half Men spent its entire series finale obsessing over Charlie, after trying to make so many other characters and storylines work since he left, goes to show that the series never moved on from Charlie. They brought in guest star Kathy Bates to play his ghost, they brought in Amber Tamblyn to play his long-lost daughter, and, of course, in the finale, they brought him back from the dead, had him vacuum up cocaine in a weird animated segment, and then pancaked him with a piano.

The whole finale just served as a reminder that they should’ve canceled Two and a Half Men after Sheen left. It’s no surprise that Lorre and Warner Bros. decided they could no longer work with Sheen, as his dependencies worsened, but they shouldn’t have tried to keep the series going without him. Kutcher was fine, but Two and a Half Men was the perfect star vehicle for Sheen, and it felt incomplete without him. The Office faced the same problem when it lost Steve Carell, but at least they got the real guy back for the finale.

Sydney Sweeney’s 104-Minute Thriller Officially Hits 275 Days on Apple TV Charts

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Coming into the year, there was plenty of anticipation for Ridley Scott’s return to the sci-fi genre with his new movie, The Dog Stars. Scott even enlisted some big-time stars such as Josh Brolin and Jacob Elordi for roles in the film, but it led to nothing more than a disappointing run at the box office.

Scott hadn’t directed a sci-fi movie since Alien: Covenant in 2017 before The Dog Stars, but a near-10-year hiatus wasn’t enough to get fans to head to theaters. Scott has a few exciting projects on the horizon, though, not least of which being his new Treasure Island reboot with Hugh Jackman. The movie, which also stars Hugh Grant, has already been set for release on November 11, 2027, and it’s expected to begin filming early next year.

In this later stage of his career, Ridley Scott may not be directing as many movies as he once was, but he’s still involved in projects everywhere thanks to his Scott Free banner. Even with the Alien franchise, Scott didn’t direct Alien: Earth or Alien: Romulus, but he did produce both projects.

Last year, Scott took his producing talents to Apple TV to work on the electric psychological thriller, Echo Valley, which stars Sydney Sweeney opposite Julianne Moore. The movie premiered on Apple TV last year in June, but it overstayed its welcome on watchlists, where it’s now been in the top 10 this year for over 275 days. It joins the ranks of other Apple TV smash hits like The Gorge and Greyhound that have managed to hit this colossal milestone this year.

Four criminal masterminds. One of them was always going to be yours. Here is who comes for you, why they picked you out of everyone available, and what they want from the encounter.

Your nemesis does not want to beat you. He wants to know you completely, and he is patient enough to wait for it. The frightening part is how good the conversation is.

Your nemesis is not after money, territory or revenge. He is after a demonstration, and you are the person he has chosen to prove his point on.

Your nemesis respects you, which sounds flattering until you work out what it means. He chose you because nobody else was worth his time.

Your nemesis has no personal interest in you whatsoever, and that is precisely what makes him unsurvivable. You are not a rival. You are a stop on the way.

Domhnall Gleeson, who can be seen leading the new Office spin-off show, The Paper, also stars alongside Sweeney and Moore in Echo Valley. The movie was written by Brad Ingelsby, who is also famous for writing two of the best HBO crime shows of the last few years, Mare of Easttown and Task. The film was directed by Michael Pearce. The official synopsis for Echo Valley, which earned scores of 53% from critics and 48% from audiences on Rotten Tomatoes, reads as follows:

“In the edge-of-your-seat thriller Echo Valley, Kate (Julianne Moore) is a mother struggling to make peace with her troubled daughter Claire (Sydney Sweeney), a situation that becomes even more perilous when Claire shows up on Kate's doorstep, hysterical and covered in someone else's blood. As Kate pieces together the shocking truth of what happened, she learns just how far a mother will go to try to save her child in this gripping tale of love, sacrifice and survival from BAFTA-winning director Michael Pearce and Emmy-nominated writer Brad Ingelsby.”

Check out Echo Valley on Apple TV and stay tuned to Collider for more updates and coverage of Ridley Scott’s future projects.

The Stock Market’s Biggest Risk Isn’t a Crash. It’s What Investors Might Do When One Arrives.

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A market decline hurts. An improvised response can make it worse.

Imagine seeing your nest egg lose 30% of its value. In this scenario, the market has already delivered a painful setback. Selling may seem like the obvious way to regain control, but selling also creates a new problem: When will you get back into the market?

In reality, panic selling -- and delaying reentering the market -- can lead to enormous opportunity costs for investors. Let's explore why.

Seeing your portfolio lose value is never fun. Indeed, psychological studies have shown that investment losses have a greater psychological impact than equivalent gains, a phenomenon known as loss aversion. Therefore, it's completely understandable that many people seek immediate relief when the market enters a sell-off, correction, or crash.

However, the psychological relief of liquidating investments creates a new problem: determining when "it's safe."

In practice, most investors simply wait too long. Many wait for recessions to end or for bullish headlines to appear. But bullish headlines often lag positive price action. As a result, investors on the sidelines often miss out on massive recovery rallies. As we'll see next, that can have a significant impact on long-term growth.

Consider a $10,000 portfolio. If it drops 30%, its value will fall to $7,000. To get back to $10,000, the portfolio will need to gain $3,000. Since the overall value has fallen, it will need to generate approximately a 43% return on $7,000 to reach $10,000. So to wipe out a 30% drop, it needs to gain 43%. These amounts differ because the starting amounts differ.

Beyond the arithmetic, there are other costs. Transaction costs, lost dividend payments, and taxes have to be considered. In short, selling might ease one type of headache but create several others.

Despite understanding the math and the other implications of selling, many investors can't resist the urge to sell when the stock market is falling. One way to train yourself not to sell is to imagine a similar situation in a different market.

For example, let's say you own a house. Now imagine that another house in your neighborhood sells for half of what might normally be expected. That sale will decrease the value of all homes in the neighborhood, perhaps significantly. But even if the price of your own home falls 10%, 15%, or even 25%, that doesn't mean one of the bedrooms in your home disappears.

Stock portfolios work the same way. Prices can fall, but that doesn't necessarily mean that the businesses behind the stocks have deteriorated to the same degree.

For a buy-and-hold strategy to work, investors must build a portfolio that can withstand periods of volatility. Leveraged positions and meme stocks can wreck a portfolio during a stock market correction or crash. Therefore, savvy investors know to limit their exposure, or, better yet, avoid such investments altogether. By building a diversified portfolio based around well-run businesses, an investor can avoid much of the worst effects of a market downturn.

Market crashes are terrible, but thankfully, they're also rare. However, to prepare for them, it's best to have a plan. Writing it down can help you manage your emotions, think carefully, and avoid panic. At the end of the day, the math and the studies are clear: It's best to ride out market downturns rather than sell and try to time reentry perfectly.

If you can keep a cool head and remember this in the moment, you'll likely come through the next financial downturn in better shape than many other investors.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 823%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 209% for the S&P 500.

Dirty ice around young star Beta Pictoris suggests frequent collisions and sheds light on the system's formation

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

In a recent study of infrared light collected by the James Webb Space Telescope (JWST), a group of astronomers detected ice for the first time around the young star Beta Pictoris. Researchers had expected ice around Beta Pictoris, but previous searches had not directly detected it. The study, published in Science, found that the ice is mostly noncrystalline and mixed with carbon dioxide. The team says these new details provide clues about the early formation of the Beta Pictoris system and collisions that may still be occurring.

10 Party Members I Benched Immediately (and Still Feel Guilty About)

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Contributor at DualShockers, former Esports Insider DACH content lead, former Escapist Magazine Germany content lead, software engineering background.

I have a complicated relationship with the party menu. In any RPG with more companions than party slots, somebody has to stay behind at camp, and whilst I would love to tell you that I rotate everyone fairly, that would be a lie. Some characters get benched within minutes of joining, and most of them never make it back out.

Whether it's through mid-battle character switching or something a little more esoteric, these JRPGs all make you rethink your party mid-battle!

It is rarely personal. Some of them are too slow, some arrive too late, and some simply have the misfortune of standing next to a character I like more. I do still feel guilty about every single one of them, though, so let’s take a look at ten party members I benched immediately, and why I’m still a little bit sorry about it.

Baldur’s Gate 3 is a very interesting case when it comes to companions: a lot of them start out really, really unlikable. Then you get to know them and their motivations, and it suddenly becomes quite understandable why they act the way that they do. Larian Studios did that so well that benching anyone feels a little bit like abandoning a friend.

Gale is the one I leave at camp regardless. I don’t mind his storyline at all, but I simply don’t enjoy the way wizards work, with every spell slot spent bringing you one step closer to yet another long rest. I usually play a Warlock myself, and having a reliable cantrip like Eldritch Blast means that I never have to worry about that kind of balancing act, especially early on.

It also doesn’t help that there are plenty of companions I like a lot more. Gale’s personality is just a bit goofy for my taste, and whilst I did keep him around for one full playthrough to properly get to know him and his story, I have never felt the need to repeat that. He is a perfectly lovely man. He’s just a perfectly lovely man who waits at camp.

Blue mages have always been one of the more interesting concepts in Final Fantasy. Their magic tends to be really powerful, since there has to be a reason to use them over your regular black, white and even red mages, and that usually means they go from being a bit weak to being suddenly wildly overpowered. Kimahri should be exactly that kind of character.

The problem is that his blue magic is tied to his Overdrive. He learns enemy abilities with Lancet, but can only use them as his Ronso Rage, and whilst there are ways to charge Overdrives faster later on, it makes him awkward for a large part of the game. Everyone else in the party already has a clearly defined role, too, and Kimahri sits in his own odd little corner of the Sphere Grid without one.

Now, I tend to 100% every Final Fantasy, so he does get used eventually. If I’m simply building a party, though, he is the last character on my mind, which is a shame. Truth be told, the story doesn’t help, either: as Yuna’s guardian, he barely speaks early on, and that makes it hard to form an emotional connection with him. His character design is absolutely sick, mind you.

Sharla is a trap, and I say that with love. When she first joins in Xenoblade Chronicles, she is a pretty good character: a dedicated healer with some decent ether arts, at a point where you have nobody else to bench her for. That is precisely where the problem starts.

Because she feels essential early on, you keep her, even as better options join. She deals next to no damage, though, which means that fights take longer, and longer fights mean that you take more damage. All of that damage then gets healed by Sharla, who ends up looking far more useful than she actually is.

I fell for it on my first playthrough, and I have seen plenty of others do exactly the same. A dedicated healer just isn’t all that necessary once you double down on damage or find more flexible healing elsewhere, like Shulk’s Light Heal. Sharla has been on my bench ever since, and I do feel bad about it, since none of this is really her fault.

From controversial clowns to big blue lionmen, Final Fantasy has party members you remember for being utterly useless but who are anything but!

For the longest time, I was shackled by what party composition makes sense for the story, and I still struggle with letting go of that sometimes. You will never catch me playing a Pokémon game without my starter, for example. In a similar vein, many of my Tales of Symphonia playthroughs simply ended up with Lloyd, Colette, Raine and Genis, because they are the ones who have known each other from the very beginning.

Eventually, I managed to get over myself. Since then, without fail, Genis has never been in my party again. I like the conversations between him and Lloyd, and I do believe that pairing a carefree character with a snarkier, more considerate one works out really well.

I just usually find him a bit grating. That is a harsh thing to say about a child who has been through as much as Genis has, and I’m aware of that every time I move him out of the party. It hasn’t stopped me yet.

Kongol always ends up benched. It’s common knowledge that Rose falls off quite aggressively on Disc 2, and again on Disc 4, which is unfortunate, but even she has seen more action in my playthroughs than he has. For the last surviving Giganto, that has to sting.

My issue is simple: I don’t usually enjoy very slow characters, and Kongol is incredibly strong but painfully slow. To make him properly useful, you have to boost both his speed and his magic defence quite aggressively, and I would rather spend that effort on someone who doesn’t need it. He never stood much of a chance with me.

Luckily, The Legend of Dragoon isn’t a particularly hard game at the best of times, so you can beat it with any party you like. That takes some of the guilt away. Kongol saved Dart and his friends from a falling pillar before joining them, though, and I repaid him with the better part of four discs spent sitting around.

It pains me so much to say this, because I love dogs! In Dragon Age: Origins, however, Dog is close to useless compared to anyone else. Unless you install a mod, he takes up a full party slot, just like any other companion would, and he can’t carry a fight the way they can.

He does a semi-decent job in the first few levels, since Alistair and Sten aren’t great tanks at that stage, either. He then gets outscaled quickly, as your warriors pick up their defensive talents and proper tank gear, and on Nightmare difficulty, that gap becomes very hard to ignore. Dog isn’t beefy enough to be a tank and doesn’t deal enough damage to make up for it, which leaves him without a clear role.

His Overwhelm ability is devastating against a single dangerous target, mages in particular, but that is about it. It’s a real shame, because his interactions with the other companions are often hilarious. Leaving him at camp means missing out on those, and it never gets any easier.

What is interesting, though, is that there exists a Extra Dog Slot mod that lets him tag along as a fifth party member, and it shows up on nearly every list of essential mods for the game. I am clearly not the only one with a guilty conscience.

Goofy has done nothing wrong, and I want that on record. My problem with the first Kingdom Hearts is that nearly every world offers a guest character who can take Donald’s or Goofy’s place, and I love how unique that makes each world feel. Somebody has to make room, and it was usually Goofy.

Recently, I felt really bad about that, and I wanted to make it right in my latest playthrough. Sadly, all it did was make me realise that the guest characters are a big part of the game’s charm for me. Fighting alongside Ariel or the Beast is simply too cool to pass up, and since they can’t leave their home worlds, it always feels like now or never.

So Goofy might have to sit this one out a little longer. He will take it well, because he is Goofy. That only makes it worse.

With a single action or word, there are moments in these Nintendo DS JRPGs Where Companions Can Derail the Entire Story.

Jean is the most painful example of wasted potential I can think of. His design is memorable, his personality is fun, and being the frog prince of SimaFort sets him apart from everyone else in Breath of Fire II. He believes that there are no bad people in the world, only bad hearts, and he says all of it in a faux-French accent.

Mechanically, sadly, he has very little to offer. He joins slow and underwhelming, with good defence but incredibly low agility, and his stats never match the way he looks. His Holy fusion turns him into a tall, heroic-looking frog knight who can in theory become quite formidable, but it comes too late and with too many trade-offs to justify his slot over characters who have already proven themselves.

Jean isn’t unplayable. He is a “what could have been”: a character who looks like he should be iconic in battle, but never gets the chance to live up to it. If he had been a little more like Frog in Chrono Trigger, with some healing to offer, he might have been worthwhile well before the endgame.

Yangus is easily one of the most lovable characters in Dragon Quest VIII, and I still barely use him for long stretches of the game. He is the dedicated tank, and the job gradually overwhelms him: magic can lower his armour, most of his equipment reduces his evasion, and he is very slow. He has a respectable Strength base, but not much gets built on top of it.

In the 3DS version, which added Red and Morrie as party members, that makes him the obvious candidate for the sidelines once the new recruits join. He can become useful again in the late game, but that means building his Scythe tree from the ground up. That is a lot to ask for a character I have already replaced.

It isn’t all doom and gloom, since he simply dips in the mid-game. Early on, 16 skill points in Humanity get you Nose for Treasure, which tells you how many chests are left in an area, and that is quite useful. I just wish that was enough to keep him in the party.

Nino joins with such a good story that you really want to use her and help her grow. Raised by the Black Fang, she is sent to assassinate Prince Zephiel in Battle Before Dawn, has a change of heart and ends up fleeing with Jaffar instead. By the time she joins you, it’s impossible not to root for her.

Unfortunately, she arrives as a Level 5 Mage very late in the game, when you are most likely fielding promoted units. Her growth rates are amongst the highest in the game, so she will catch up if you put the time in, but she’s a glass cannon until then, and there isn’t much game left. Unless you wrap your whole strategy around her, she gets benched immediately. This makes her a classic example of what Fire Emblem fans call the Est archetype: a unit that joins late and underlevelled, but with enormous potential.

Sometimes, it feels like her sole purpose is to stand between you and recruiting Jaffar, since he only joins if she talks to him and they both survive. This tiny mage is brimming with potential, and I have made use of it in some playthroughs. I’m just not always in the mood for that, so more often than not, she ends up on the bench.

Crisp air, hot coffee and a cozy sofa: autumn is when I reach for nostalgia, and these ten GameCube JRPGs still make the trip back worthwhile.

8 Soulslikes With Insane Difficulty Spikes

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Christian Bognar is a Contributor at DualShockers with over three years of experience covering the gaming industry. Since 2022, he has written guides and reviews for publications including DualShockers, CBR, Attack of the Fanboy, PC Invasion, and The Game Slayer. He holds a Bachelor’s Degree in Business Administration

The Soulslikes genre offers some of the greatest challenges in gaming. With difficult bosses, enemies that demand patience, and many obstacles throughout a level, meeting success in any Soulslike can be incredibly rewarding.

However, there are times when a game takes it too far and includes a harsh difficulty spike that seemingly comes out of nowhere. This could sometimes lead to areas of the game feeling a bit unfair.

It's not the genre's area of expertise, but some of its representatives are more daring, offering fascinating movement mechanics.

While the games listed in this article are some of the best entries in the genre, they do have pacing issues when it comes to difficulty. Here are eight Soulslikes with brutal difficulty spikes.

Sekiro is already a demanding game from the get-go, but its difficulty reaches a whole other level in the mid to late game. Enemies become more aggressive, minibosses hit harder, and all encounters will constantly remind players that they need to master the parry system to stand a chance.

Boss fights also become considerably more demanding, requiring players to learn lengthy attack patterns and maintain constant pressure rather than relying on dodging. Just when you thought you were getting good at the game, Sekiro: Shadows Die Twice raises the bar.

The expansion of Elden Ring, Shadow of the Erdtree, is known for its difficulty spike right from the start. As soon as you enter the Land of Shadow, you’ll realize enemies deal way more damage, and your character is much weaker overall.

With the introduction of Scadutree Fragments, items that raise attack power and defense, players essentially have to start from scratch and engage with a new progression system. In other words, even OP base-game builds get a wake-up call when starting the DLC.

Overall, Lies of P is generally on the easier side for a Soulslike. However, the game has brutal difficulty spikes in a handful of its bosses. For example, Laxasia the Complete, Simon Manus, and King of Puppets are ten times more challenging than anything else in the game.

Each of these bosses is a true test of reflexes and endurance, where even the smallest mistake can prove costly. What makes these encounters particularly jarring is the stark contrast in difficulty, as the areas leading up to them are relatively easy to breeze through.

There’s no denying that The First Berserker: Khazan is a very challenging game right from its first level. Still, despite it being a very tough experience through and through, it still features an insane difficulty spike in its second half that can sometimes feel pretty unfair.

Late-game bosses are some of the most demanding encounters the genre has to offer, featuring relentless combos that leave very little room for mistakes. Khazan’s combat is super satisfying, but players who haven’t mastered its mechanics will quickly discover just how unforgiving it can be.

If you're looking for the heir to Soulsborne games, look no further than this list of ten Soulslikes that are unexpected masterpieces.

While the 2023 version of Lords of the Fallen never reaches the difficulty heights of 2014’s, it’s definitely not an easy experience. The game reaches its peak in difficulty during Bramis Castle, an area filled with the hardest enemies in the entire game.

Bramis Castle has been heavily regarded as unfair by many players due to the absurd amount of elite enemies. It was so hard that there were multiple post-launch patches to make it feel more reasonable. Still, despite the patches to make it easier, Bramis Castle remains an insane difficulty spike.

The original Hollow Knight was a fairly well-balanced experience from beginning to end, and while Silksong is a phenomenal game, it’s not as consistent as the original. For example, Bilewater increases the difficulty tenfold due to poisonous waters and brutal platforming.

Additionally, Act 3 of Silksong introduces an entirely new set of challenges as the Void corrupts Pharloom, making enemies considerably more dangerous while changing areas and levels. Fortunately, Act 3 is completely optional, meaning players who aren’t interested in tackling this difficulty spike can simply ignore it.

Wuchang: Fallen Feathers features several notoriously sharp difficulty spikes, mainly in its boss fights. Wuchang has the tendency to make the player feel incredibly powerful while exploring a level and then completely ripping that feeling away during some of the main bosses.

One of the earliest examples of this is the Commander Honglan boss fight, the first brick wall players will hit in a playthrough. All earlier bosses are a walk in the park compared to Commander Honglan, and everything that follows involves bosses that demand fast reflexes and perfect performance.

Nioh 3's difficulty spikes can be particularly unforgiving, especially when players enter the Heian Period. This period involves some of the most challenging locations and bosses in the game, such as Ibaraki Doji, Kajiwara Kagetoki, and many more.

Pretty much, once the player enters the Heian Period, the real Nioh 3 experience begins, and the rest of the adventure is a true test of skill. Bosses hit hard, are faster, and the importance of leveling equipment increases dramatically.

There's nothing more fitting for this season than being destroyed by the same boss fifty-seven times in a row.
